tracking whether I understand the pattern.\n- key: S020\n  name: Hallucinations\n  description: Perception-like experiences that occur without an external stimulus and are as vivid and clear as normal perception.\n  manifestation: Sometimes when I am alone at my desk or walking home from class, I hear a man's voice say things like 'check line seven' or 'you missed the sign,' clear enough that I look around even though nobody is there.\n- key: S021\n  name: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\n  description: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.\n  manifestation: When I try to explain why a ledger is off, my thoughts jump from tuition deadlines to tax rules to studio lighting and number patterns, and people tell me they cannot follow how I got from one point to the next.\n- key: S022\n  name: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\n  description: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.\n  manifestation: I came in because I have started doing things at work and school that do not make sense, like lining receipts across the floor, tapping the calculator in sets of nine, or standing by the copier with my hands held up until someone asks if I am okay.\n- key: S023\n  name: Negative_Symptoms\n  description: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.\n  manifestation: I still care about people, but most days I let texts sit unanswered, skip hanging out after class, and feel flat even when friends talk about things I used to enjoy, like music shows or art projects.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: Some weeks I feel unusually sharp and almost unstoppable, like I can map out my whole future in one night, but then a small correction from my boss or a classmate can make me snap at them harder than I mean to.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: When that energy comes on, I cannot sit through a normal bookkeeping shift without pacing, opening extra tabs, reorganizing files, or feeling like my hands need to keep moving.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: I have gone several nights sleeping only two or three hours and still felt ready to go to work and class, like sleep was just taking time away from getting everything figured out.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I will be trying to reconcile one account, but a printer noise, a blinking cursor, or someone whispering in the hallway pulls me off task and suddenly I am checking something completely different.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I keep starting big plans all at once, like building templates for every client, emailing classmates about a study group, and sketching out a bookkeeping service for small art studios before finishing the work already due.\n- key: S041\n  name: Painful_Consequence_Behavior\n  description: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.\n  manifestation: I spent more than I could afford on accounting software, art equipment, and a deposit for a trip to meet studio owners because at the time it felt like the obvious next step and I did not really think about rent or my card balance.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During the weeks when I was barely sleeping and felt driven to start all those plans, I was also hearing the voice and feeling sure the invoice numbers were sending me messages.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: Looking back, the wired, overconfident or irritable stretches have taken up more of this whole period than the calmer stretches, even when the intensity rose and fell.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when I was sleeping more normally and did not feel especially energized, but I still heard the voice and felt pulled into the number patterns.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I failed an important course module, my ledgers started coming back with serious errors, and I have had to ask for help because I cannot reliably finish work that used to be routine for me.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 10 months\n  manifestation: This has been building for about 10 months, since I started the evening accounting courses while keeping my bookkeeping job.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 8 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the feeling that numbers are messages have been happening for about 8 months now.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 6 months\n  manifestation: The high-energy, little-sleep periods have been coming and going for roughly 6 In my head it sort of connects, but other people tell me they can’t follow how I got from one point to the next.\n\nDoctor: When you stand still with your hands held up, does it feel like your body gets stuck in that position and is hard to move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. Like I’ll be standing by the copier with my hands kind of held up, and I just stay there longer than makes sense until someone asks if I’m okay. It’s not always like I physically can’t move, but I feel kind of stuck and I don’t snap out of it right away.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that you resist being moved, or that you stay in an unusual posture for a long time even after someone tries to change it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve resisted someone moving me, exactly. But yeah, I’ve stayed in weird positions too long, like standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay. And I’ve been doing other stuff that doesn’t really make sense, like lining receipts across the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen someone tries to move your arms or body while you’re stuck in one of those positions, do you stay that way or resist being moved?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I just kind of end up doing these things, like lining the receipts up or tapping the calculator in that pattern, and it doesn’t really make sense to me afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. If I’m alone at my desk, or walking home from class, I’ll hear a man’s voice say something like, “check line seven,” or “you missed the sign.” It sounds clear enough that I turn and look around, but there’s nobody there.\n\nDoctor: Before these recent problems started, did you usually have a hard time understanding other people or making friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I was never super outgoing, but I could understand people well enough and I had a few friends. It wasn’t really a big problem before this.\n\nDoctor: Since these problems started, have you been having a harder time keeping up with your work or school than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I used to handle my schoolwork and the bookkeeping stuff pretty routinely, but lately I’m not keeping up. I failed an important course module, and at work my ledgers have been coming back with serious mistakes. I’ve had to ask for help with things I used to be able to finish on my own.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your facial expression or voice has become much less expressive lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a couple people have said I seem kind of blank or quiet. I still care about them, but most days I just… don’t really react much. I leave texts unanswered, skip hanging out after class, and even when they talk about music shows or art projects I used to be into, I feel pretty flat.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your speech has become hard to follow or jumbled?\n\nPatient: Yeah, people have said that. Like at work, if I’m trying to explain why a ledger is off, I’ll start with the numbers, then suddenly I’m talking about tuition deadlines or tax rules, or even something like studio lighting and patterns in the numbers. In my head it sort of connects, but other people tell me they can’t follow how I got from one point to the next.\n\nDoctor: When you stand still with your hands held up, does it feel like your body gets stuck in that position and is hard to move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. Like I’ll be standing by the copier with my hands kind of held up, and I just stay there longer than makes sense until someone asks if I’m okay. It’s not always like I physically can’t move, but I feel kind of stuck and I don’t snap out of it right away.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that you resist being moved, or that you stay in an unusual posture for a long time even after someone tries to change it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve resisted someone moving me, exactly. But yeah, I’ve stayed in weird positions too long, like standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay. And I’ve been doing other stuff that doesn’t really make sense, like lining receipts across the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arms or body while you’re stuck in one of those positions, do you stay that way or resist being moved?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure if I resist being moved. I don’t think anyone’s really tried to move my arms much. What I notice is I’ll just stay there too long, like by the copier with my hands up, until someone says something. And I’ve been doing other odd stuff too, like lining receipts on the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone ever placed your arm or body into a position and found that you stayed that way after they let go?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has actually put my arm in a position like that and checked. What’s been happening is more like I end up doing odd things on my own, like standing by the copier with my hands up too long until someone asks if I’m okay, or lining receipts on the floor, or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone been able to place one of your arms into a position and found that it stayed there after they let go?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t think anyone’s tried placing my arm like that and then letting go to see if it stayed.\n\nDoctor: When someone moves your arm into a position, does it tend to stay there after they let go?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that I’ll get stuck doing things that don’t really make sense, like standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay, or lining receipts across the floor.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I just kind of end up doing these things, like lining the receipts up or tapping the calculator in that pattern, and it doesn’t really make sense to me afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. If I’m alone at my desk, or walking home from class, I’ll hear a man’s voice say something like, “check line seven,” or “you missed the sign.” It sounds clear enough that I turn and look around, but there’s nobody there.\n\nDoctor: Before these recent problems started, did you usually have a hard time understanding other people or making friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I was never super outgoing, but I could understand people well enough and I had a few friends. It wasn’t really a big problem before this.\n\nDoctor: Since these problems started, have you been having a harder time keeping up with your work or school than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I used to handle my schoolwork and the bookkeeping stuff pretty routinely, but lately I’m not keeping up. I failed an important course module, and at work my ledgers have been coming back with serious mistakes. I’ve had to ask for help with things I used to be able to finish on my own.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your facial expression or voice has become much less expressive lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a couple people have said I seem kind of blank or quiet. I still care about them, but most days I just… don’t really react much. I leave texts unanswered, skip hanging out after class, and even when they talk about music shows or art projects I used to be into, I feel pretty flat.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your speech has become hard to follow or jumbled?\n\nPatient: Yeah, people have said that. Like at work, if I’m trying to explain why a ledger is off, I’ll start with the numbers, then suddenly I’m talking about tuition deadlines or tax rules, or even something like studio lighting and patterns in the numbers. In my head it sort of connects, but other people tell me they can’t follow how I got from one point to the next.\n\nDoctor: When you stand still with your hands held up, does it feel like your body gets stuck in that position and is hard to move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. Like I’ll be standing by the copier with my hands kind of held up, and I just stay there longer than makes sense until someone asks if I’m okay. It’s not always like I physically can’t move, but I feel kind of stuck and I don’t snap out of it right away.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that you resist being moved, or that you stay in an unusual posture for a long time even after someone tries to change it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve resisted someone moving me, exactly. But yeah, I’ve stayed in weird positions too long, like standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay. And I’ve been doing other stuff that doesn’t really make sense, like lining receipts across the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arms or body while you’re stuck in one of those positions, do you stay that way or resist being moved?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure if I resist being moved. I don’t think anyone’s really tried to move my arms much. What I notice is I’ll just stay there too long, like by the copier with my hands up, until someone says something. And I’ve been doing other odd stuff too, like lining receipts on the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone ever placed your arm or body into a position and found that you stayed that way after they let go?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has actually put my arm in a position like that and checked. What’s been happening is more like I end up doing odd things on my own, like standing by the copier with my hands up too long until someone asks if I’m okay, or lining receipts on the floor, or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone been able to place one of your arms into a position and found that it stayed there after they let go?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t think anyone’s tried placing my arm like that and then letting go to see if it stayed.\n\nDoctor: When someone moves your arm into a position, does it tend to stay there after they let go?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that I’ll get stuck doing things that don’t really make sense, like standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay, or lining receipts across the floor.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to guide or reposition you, do you find yourself resisting them even when you don’t mean to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really notice myself resisting people moving me, no. The problem is more that I’ll start doing things that don’t make sense, like lining receipts on the floor or tapping the calculator in patterns, and I don’t snap out of it right away.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you stopped speaking entirely, even when people were talking to you or expecting an answer?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve stopped speaking entirely like that. I might get quiet or take a bit to answer, but I don’t remember just not talking at all when someone was asking me something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s not always like I physically can’t move, but I feel kind of stuck and I don’t snap out of it right away.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that you resist being moved, or that you stay in an unusual posture for a long time even after someone tries to change it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve resisted someone moving me, exactly. But yeah, I’ve stayed in weird positions too long, like standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay. And I’ve been doing other stuff that doesn’t really make sense, like lining receipts across the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arms or body while you’re stuck in one of those positions, do you stay that way or resist being moved?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure if I resist being moved. I don’t think anyone’s really tried to move my arms much. What I notice is I’ll just stay there too long, like by the copier with my hands up, until someone says something. And I’ve been doing other odd stuff too, like lining receipts on the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone ever placed your arm or body into a position and found that you stayed that way after they let go?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has actually put my arm in a position like that and checked. What’s been happening is more like I end up doing odd things on my own, like standing by the copier with my hands up too long until someone asks if I’m okay, or lining receipts on the floor, or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone been able to place one of your arms into a position and found that it stayed there after they let go?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t think anyone’s tried placing my arm like that and then letting go to see if it stayed.\n\nDoctor: When someone moves your arm into a position, does it tend to stay there after they let go?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that I’ll get stuck doing things that don’t really make sense, like standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay, or lining receipts across the floor.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to guide or reposition you, do you find yourself resisting them even when you don’t mean to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really notice myself resisting people moving me, no. The problem is more that I’ll start doing things that don’t make sense, like lining receipts on the floor or tapping the calculator in patterns, and I don’t snap out of it right away.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you stopped speaking entirely, even when people were talking to you or expecting an answer?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve stopped speaking entirely like that. I might get quiet or take a bit to answer, but I don’t remember just not talking at all when someone was asking me something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever repeat another person’s words exactly right after they say them?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I repeat people’s words back right after they say them. I don’t think anyone’s really tried to move my arms much. What I notice is I’ll just stay there too long, like by the copier with my hands up, until someone says something. And I’ve been doing other odd stuff too, like lining receipts on the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone ever placed your arm or body into a position and found that you stayed that way after they let go?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has actually put my arm in a position like that and checked. The problem is more that I’ll start doing things that don’t make sense, like lining receipts on the floor or tapping the calculator in patterns, and I don’t snap out of it right away.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you stopped speaking entirely, even when people were talking to you or expecting an answer?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve stopped speaking entirely like that. I might get quiet or take a bit to answer, but I don’t remember just not talking at all when someone was asking me something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever repeat another person’s words exactly right after they say them?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I repeat people’s words back right after they say them. It’s more that I end up doing odd things, like lining receipts across the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine without really meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you became very physically restless or active in a way that seemed purposeless, like pacing or moving around a lot for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I used to handle my schoolwork and the bookkeeping stuff pretty routinely, but lately I’m not keeping up. I failed an important course module, and at work my ledgers have been coming back with serious mistakes. I’ve had to ask for help with things I used to be able to finish on my own.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your facial expression or voice has become much less expressive lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a couple people have said I seem kind of blank or quiet. I still care about them, but most days I just… don’t really react much. I leave texts unanswered, skip hanging out after class, and even when they talk about music shows or art projects I used to be into, I feel pretty flat.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your speech has become hard to follow or jumbled?\n\nPatient: Yeah, people have said that. Like at work, if I’m trying to explain why a ledger is off, I’ll start with the numbers, then suddenly I’m talking about tuition deadlines or tax rules, or even something like studio lighting and patterns in the numbers. In my head it sort of connects, but other people tell me they can’t follow how I got from one point to the next.\n\nDoctor: When you stand still with your hands held up, does it feel like your body gets stuck in that position and is hard to move?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. Like I’ll be standing by the copier with my hands kind of held up, and I just stay there longer than makes sense until someone asks if I’m okay. It’s not always like I physically can’t move, but I feel kind of stuck and I don’t snap out of it right away.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that you resist being moved, or that you stay in an unusual posture for a long time even after someone tries to change it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve resisted someone moving me, exactly. But yeah, I’ve stayed in weird positions too long, like standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay. And I’ve been doing other stuff that doesn’t really make sense, like lining receipts across the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to move your arms or body while you’re stuck in one of those positions, do you stay that way or resist being moved?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure if I resist being moved. I don’t think anyone’s really tried to move my arms much. What I notice is I’ll just stay there too long, like by the copier with my hands up, until someone says something. And I’ve been doing other odd stuff too, like lining receipts on the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone ever placed your arm or body into a position and found that you stayed that way after they let go?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has actually put my arm in a position like that and checked. What’s been happening is more like I end up doing odd things on my own, like standing by the copier with my hands up too long until someone asks if I’m okay, or lining receipts on the floor, or tapping the calculator in sets of nine.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone been able to place one of your arms into a position and found that it stayed there after they let go?\n\nPatient: No, not that I know of. I don’t think anyone’s tried placing my arm like that and then letting go to see if it stayed.\n\nDoctor: When someone moves your arm into a position, does it tend to stay there after they let go?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that I’ll get stuck doing things that don’t really make sense, like standing by the copier with my hands up until someone asks if I’m okay, or lining receipts across the floor.\n\nDoctor: When someone tries to guide or reposition you, do you find yourself resisting them even when you don’t mean to?\n\nPatient: I don’t really notice myself resisting people moving me, no. The problem is more that I’ll start doing things that don’t make sense, like lining receipts on the floor or tapping the calculator in patterns, and I don’t snap out of it right away.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you stopped speaking entirely, even when people were talking to you or expecting an answer?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve stopped speaking entirely like that. I might get quiet or take a bit to answer, but I don’t remember just not talking at all when someone was asking me something.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever repeat another person’s words exactly right after they say them?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I repeat people’s words back right after they say them. It’s more that I end up doing odd things, like lining receipts across the floor or tapping the calculator in sets of nine without really meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you became very physically restless or active in a way that seemed purposeless, like pacing or moving around a lot for no clear reason?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I wouldn’t say it’s always just pacing, but I get really active with starting things. Like I’ll suddenly try to make templates for every client, email people about starting a study group, and then start planning out a bookkeeping service for small art studios, all before I’ve even finished the work that’s already due. It feels like I’m doing a lot, but it’s not really focused.\n\nDoctor: During these periods, do you become so physically agitated that you can’t seem to settle down, even when there’s nothing specific you’re trying to do?\n\nPatient: Not really like I’m just agitated with nothing to do. It’s more like I can’t stop starting things. I’ll be making templates for every client, then emailing classmates about a study group, then sketching out some bookkeeping service idea for small art studios, all before I’ve even finished the work that’s already due.\n\nDoctor: Have you been neglecting basic self-care, like bathing or changing clothes, more than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. I’m still bathing and changing clothes like usual, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever copy another person’s movements right after they do them?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I do that. I haven’t noticed myself copying someone’s movements right after they do them."
